The limit is found by simplifying the expression and evaluating at \( x = 1 \). 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Factor the Numerator**: 
   The numerator \( x^3 - 1 \) can be factored using the difference of cubes formula:
   \[
   x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
   \]

2. **Simplify the Expression**:
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression and cancel out \( (x - 1) \):
   \[
   \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1} = x^2 + x + 1 \quad (x \neq 1)
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit**:
   Now, compute the limit as \( x \) approaches 1:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The limit is **3**.

The given expression is \(\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{e^x}{x^2}\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Form:** As \( x \) approaches infinity, both \( e^x \) and \( x^2 \) tend to infinity. This results in an indeterminate form of \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), which allows us to apply L'Hospital's Rule.

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Differentiate the numerator and denominator with respect to \( x \).
     - The derivative of \( e^x \) is \( e^x \).
     - The derivative of \( x^2 \) is \( 2x \).
   - The limit becomes \( \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{e^x}{2x} \).

3. **Check the New Form:** Again, as \( x \) approaches infinity, both \( e^x \) and \( x \) tend to infinity, resulting in another indeterminate form of \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \). Apply L'Hospital's Rule once more.

4. **Second Application of L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Differentiate the numerator and denominator again.
     - The derivative of \( e^x \) remains \( e^x \).
     - The derivative of \( 2x \) is \( 2 \).
   - The limit simplifies to \( \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{e^x}{2} \).

5. **Evaluate the Limit:** As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( e^x \) grows without bound, making the entire expression tend to infinity.

**Final Answer:**
The limit is infinity because exponential functions grow much faster than polynomial functions as \( x \) approaches infinity.

First off, when dealing with rational functions like this one—where both numerator and denominator are polynomials—I think the key is to look at the highest degree terms in both. The given expression is evaluated as follows:

We are to find:
\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x
\]

This limit is a well-known result related to the mathematical constant \( e \). Specifically, it represents one of the definitions of Euler's number:

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Expression:**  
   The expression \(\left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x\) as \( x \) approaches infinity is a standard limit in calculus.

2. **Recognize the Limit:**  
   As \( x \to \infty \), \( \frac{1}{x} \) becomes very small, approaching zero. Thus, \( 1 + \frac{1}{x} \) approaches 1 from above.

3. **Apply Known Limit:**  
   The limit of the form \( (1 + \frac{a}{n})^n \) as \( n \to \infty \) is known to be \( e^a \). Here, \( a = 1 \), so the limit becomes \( e^1 = e \).

4. **Conclusion:**  
   Therefore, the value of the given expression's limit is Euler's number.

So now the limit becomes lim x→∞ (2x +1)/(2x -1). Again, both numerator and denominator are linear functions with degree 1. So applying L’Hospital again might be necessary since plugging in infinity would still give ∞/∞.

Taking derivatives once more: The derivative of 2x +1 is 2, and the derivative of 2x -1 is also 2. Now the limit simplifies to lim x→∞ (2)/(2) = 1.

So using L’Hospital’s Rule twice still leads me to the same answer, which reinforces that the limit is indeed 1.

Alternatively, another method I recall is factoring out the highest power of x from both numerator and denominator. Let's try that approach as well for confirmation.

In the numerator: x² +x +1 = x²(1 + 1/x + 1/x²).
Denominator: x² -x +1 = x²(1 - 1/x + 1/x²).

So, factoring out x² gives (x²(1 + 1/x + 1/x²))/(x²(1 - 1/x + 1/x²)). The x² terms cancel out since they're the same in numerator and denominator, leaving us with [1 + 1/x + 1/x²]/[1 - 1/x + 1/x²]. As before, when we take the limit as x approaches infinity, all those terms with 1/x go to zero, so it's (1+0+0)/(1-0+0) = 1/1 = 1.

Hmm, this is consistent across different methods. So regardless of whether I use direct substitution after dividing by highest power, L’Hospital’s Rule twice, or factoring out the leading term, I get the same result: limit as x approaches infinity of (x² +x +1)/(x² -x +1) equals 1.

I think it's pretty solid that the answer is indeed 1. There doesn't seem to be any step where I could have made a mistake or overlooked something since multiple methods confirm the same result.
</think>

The limit as x approaches infinity of (x² + x + 1)/(x² - x + 1) is found by considering the leading terms in both numerator and denominator. Both are quadratic polynomials with the same degree, so their ratio tends to the quotient of their coefficients, which is 1/1 = 1.

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 1 of the expression \( \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - (1 + 1^3)^{1/3}}{x - 1} \), we can use the concept of derivatives.

First, we recognize that this limit is in the form of the derivative definition:
\[
\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}
\]
where \( f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \) and \( a = 1 \).

We need to find the derivative of \( f(x) \) at \( x = 1 \). The function is:
\[
f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3}
\]

Using the chain rule, we compute the derivative:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = x^2 (1 + x^3)^{-2/3}
\]

Evaluating this derivative at \( x = 1 \):
\[
f'(1) = 1^2 (1 + 1^3)^{-2/3} = 1 \cdot (2)^{-2/3} = 2^{-2/3}
\]

Simplifying \( 2^{-2/3} \), we get:
\[
2^{-2/3} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}} = \frac{1}{(2^{1/3})^2} = \frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}
\]

Thus, the limit is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{\sqrt[3]{4}}}
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will use basic differentiation rules. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

1. **Differentiate each term separately**:
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 2 \cdot 3x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
   
   - The derivative of \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives**:
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

3. **Final Answer**:
   \[
   f'(x) = \boxed{6x - 5}
   \]

The problem requires finding the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \).

First, rewrite \( \frac{1}{x} \) as \( x^{-1} \). Applying the power rule:

\[

\[

The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is found using the fundamental rule of calculus.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Function:** The function given is \( e^x \), where \( e \) is the base of the natural logarithm and \( x \) is the variable.
2. **Apply the Derivative Rule:** The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is a well-known result in calculus: 
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = e^x
   \]
3. **Conclusion:** Since the exponent is simply \( x \), there's no additional term or modification needed.

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\boxed{e^{x}}

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \), we will apply basic differentiation rules to each term individually.

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Differentiate \( x^3 \):**
   Using the power rule, which states that \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = nx^{n-1} \),
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} x^3 = 3x^2
   \]

2. **Differentiate \( 2x^2 \):**
   Applying the power rule,
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (2x^2) = 4x
   \]
   
3. **Differentiate \( x \):**
   The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} x = 1
   \]

4. **Differentiate the constant term \( 1 \):**
   The derivative of any constant is 0.
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} 1 = 0
   \]

5. **Combine all the derivatives:**
   Adding up all the individual derivatives,
   \[
   f'(x) = 3x^2 + 4x + 1
   \]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{\frac{d}{dx} (x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1) = 3x^2 + 4x + 1}
\]

The problem requires finding the derivative of \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^x) \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let y = x^x**
   - Define \( y = x^x \) to simplify differentiation.

2. **Take Natural Logarithm on Both Sides**
   - Apply natural logarithm: \( \ln(y) = \ln(x^x) \).
   - Use logarithmic identity: \( \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x) \).

3. **Differentiate Both Sides with Respect to x**
   - Differentiate left side using chain rule: \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \).
   - Differentiate right side using product rule:
     - Let \( u = x \), so \( u' = 1 \).
     - Let \( v = \ln(x) \), so \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \).
     - Apply product rule: \( u'v + uv' = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \).

4. **Solve for dy/dx**
   - Multiply both sides by y: \( \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1) \).
   - Substitute back \( y = x^x \): \( \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1) \).

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( x^x(\ln(x) + 1) \).

**Product Rule:**  
If you have a function \( f(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then the derivative \( f'(x) \) is given by:
\[ f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x) \]

   - Let \( u(x) = x^2 \)
   - Let \( v(x) = e^x \)

2. **Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   - \( u'(x) = 2x \) (using the power rule)
   - \( v'(x) = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( e^x \) is itself)

   \[
   f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]

   \[

   \]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the chain rule. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

**Step 1:** Identify the outer and inner functions.
- **Outer function:** Natural logarithm, which is \( \ln(u) \)
- **Inner function:** \( u = \sin(x) \)

**Step 2:** Differentiate the outer function with respect to the inner function.
\[
\frac{d}{du} [\ln(u)] = \frac{1}{u}
\]

**Step 3:** Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \).
\[
\frac{d}{dx} [\sin(x)] = \cos(x)
\]

**Step 4:** Multiply the derivatives from Step 2 and Step 3.
\[
F'(x) = \frac{1}{u} \cdot \cos(x) = \frac{\cos(x)}{\sin(x)} = \cot(x)
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
F'(x) = \boxed{\cot(x)}
\]

The given function is \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \).

To find its derivative with respect to \( x \), we apply the chain rule:

1. Let \( u = x^2 \).
2. Then, \( F(u) = e^u \).
3. The derivative of \( F(u) \) with respect to \( u \) is \( \frac{dF}{du} = e^u \).
4. The derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{du}{dx} = 2x \).

Applying the chain rule:

\[
\frac{dF}{dx} = \frac{dF}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = e^u \cdot 2x
\]

Substituting back \( u = x^2 \):

\[
\frac{dF}{dx} = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. 

The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Let's identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u(x) = x \quad \text{and} \quad v(x) = 1 + x^2
\]

Now, find their derivatives:
\[
u'(x) = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad v'(x) = 2x
\]

Apply the quotient rule:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator:
\[
= \frac{1 + x^2 - 2x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2} = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) \) is:
\[
F'(x) = \boxed{\dfrac{1 - x^{2}}{(1 + x^{2})^{2}}}
\]

\]

\[

\]

To find the limit of the given expression as \( x \) approaches infinity, let's analyze it step by step.

**Given Expression:**
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{3x - \ln x}{5x + \ln x}
\]

**Step 1:** Observe the behavior of each term as \( x \) becomes very large.
- The terms \( 3x \) and \( 5x \) are linear in \( x \).
- The logarithmic terms \( \ln x \) grow much slower than linear terms.

**Step 2:** Divide both the numerator and the denominator by \( x \) to simplify:
\[
\frac{3x - \ln x}{5x + \ln x} = \frac{3 - \frac{\ln x}{x}}{5 + \frac{\ln x}{x}}
\]

**Step 3:** Evaluate the limits of each part as \( x \to \infty \):
- \( \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\ln x}{x} = 0 \) because the logarithmic function grows slower than the linear function.

**Step 4:** Substitute these limits back into the expression:
\[
\frac{3 - 0}{5 + 0} = \frac{3}{5}
\]

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\tan x}{x} \) can be evaluated by recognizing that \( \tan x = \sin x / \cos x \). Thus, we rewrite the expression and apply known limits.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Rewrite the Expression:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\tan x}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x / \cos x}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \left( \frac{\sin x}{x} \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \right)
   \]

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x} \) can be found using the Taylor series expansion or L'Hospital's Rule. 

Using the Taylor series:
\[ \ln(1+x) = x - \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^3}{3} - \dots \]
Dividing by \( x \):
\[ \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = 1 - \frac{x}{2} + \frac{x^2}{3} - \dots \]
As \( x \to 0 \), the higher power terms vanish, leaving:
\[ \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = 1 \]

Using L'Hospital's Rule:
Differentiate numerator and denominator:
Numerator derivative: \( \frac{d}{dx}\ln(1+x) = \frac{1}{1+x} \)
Denominator derivative: \( \frac{d}{dx}x = 1 \)
Thus, the limit becomes:
\[ \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1} = \frac{1}{1+0} = 1 \]

Both methods confirm that the limit is 1.

The limit as x approaches infinity of (1 + 1/x)^x is a well-known expression in calculus that evaluates to Euler's number, e.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Recognize the Standard Limit**: The given expression resembles the standard limit formula for e:
   
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x = e
   \]

2. **Take Natural Logarithm**: To evaluate this, let’s take the natural logarithm (ln) of both sides:

   Let \( y = \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x \)

   Then,
   
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln\left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)
   \]

3. **Expand Using Taylor Series**: For small values of \( t \), we can approximate \( \ln(1 + t) \) using the Taylor series expansion around 0:

   \[
   \ln(1 + t) \approx t - \frac{t^2}{2} + \frac{t^3}{3} - \dots
   \]

   Here, \( t = \frac{1}{x} \), so for large x,

   \[
   \ln\left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right) \approx \frac{1}{x} - \frac{1}{2x^2} + \dots
   \]

4. **Multiply by x**:

   Substitute the approximation into \( \ln(y) \):

   \[
   \ln(y) = x \left( \frac{1}{x} - \frac{1}{2x^2} + \dots \right ) = 1 - \frac{1}{2x} + \dots
   \]

5. **Take the Limit**:

   As \( x \to \infty \), the terms involving \( \frac{1}{x} \) and higher powers approach zero:

   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \ln(y) = 1 - 0 + \dots = 1
   \]

6. **Exponentiate to Solve for y**:

   Since \( \ln(y) \to 1 \), we have:
   
   \[
   y = e^1 = e
   \]

7. **Conclusion**:

   Therefore, the limit is Euler's number.

{Final Answer: "Numeric answer": "e"}

The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is found using the definition of a derivative:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^{x+h} - e^x}{h} = e^x \cdot \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h}
\]

Since \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h} = 1 \), we have:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = e^x
\]

The derivative of f(x) = tan(x) can be found using differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express tan(x):**
   - tan(x) = sin(x)/cos(x)

2. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**
   - Let u = sin(x), so du/dx = cos(x)
   - Let v = cos(x), so dv/dx = -sin(x)
   
3. **Use the Quotient Rule Formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{\cos(x)\cos(x) - (-\sin(x))\sin(x)}{\cos^2(x)}
   \]
   
4. **Simplify the Numerator:**
   \[
   \cos^2(x) + \sin^2(x) = 1
   \]
   
5. **Final Derivative:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1}{\cos^2(x)} = \sec^2(x)
   \]

**Answer:**  
The derivative of tan(x) is sec²(x).

To find the derivative \( F'(x) \), I will use the product rule, which states that \( (u \cdot v)' = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v' \).

Calculating the derivatives:
- The derivative of \( u(x) = \cos x \) is \( u'(x) = -\sin x \).
- The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln x \) is \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we will use the **product rule** in calculus. The product rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), its derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step 1: Identify the Components**

Let's identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):

\[
u(x) = \cos x
\]
\[
v(x) = \ln x
\]

**Step 2: Find the Derivatives of Each Component**

First, find the derivative of \( u(x) = \cos x \):

\[
u'(x) = -\sin x
\]

Next, find the derivative of \( v(x) = \ln x \):

\[
v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Product Rule**

Now, apply the product rule:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
F'(x) = (-\sin x) \cdot \ln x + \cos x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Expression**

Simplify the expression by combining like terms:

\[
F'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

This is the final derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \).

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \) is:

\[
F'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

So, putting it all together, the derivative of x^x with respect to x is x^x times (ln(x) plus one).
</think>

The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using logarithmic differentiation.

Let \( y = x^x \).

1. Take the natural logarithm of both sides:
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]

2. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side: \( \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot y' \)
   - Right side: Using the product rule, \( \frac{d}{dx}[x \cdot \ln(x)] = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \)

3. Combine the results:
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot y' = \ln(x) + 1
   \]

4. Solve for \( y' \):
   \[
   y' = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

5. Substitute \( y = x^x \):
   \[
   y' = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:** {Final Answer: "x^x (ln(x) + 1)"}

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^{x} \), we'll use the product rule. 

**Product Rule:**  
If \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \) are differentiable functions, then the derivative of their product is:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[u(x) \cdot v(x)] = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step 1:** Let  
\( u(x) = x \) and \( v(x) = e^{x} \)

**Step 2:** Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):  
\[

\]  
\[

\]

To find the derivative of the function:

\[
F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1}
\]

we can use the chain rule. Here's a step-by-step solution:

1. **Rewrite the Function**:  
   First, rewrite \( F(x) \) in a more convenient form for differentiation:
   
   \[
   F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Chain Rule**:  
   The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( f(g(x)) \), its derivative is \( f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \). Here, let:
   
   - Outer function: \( f(u) = u^{-1} \)
   - Inner function: \( u = g(x) = x^2 + 1 \)

3. **Differentiate the Outer Function**:  
   Differentiate \( f(u) \) with respect to \( u \):
   
   \[
   f'(u) = -u^{-2}
   \]

4. **Differentiate the Inner Function**:  
   Differentiate \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \) with respect to \( x \):
   
   \[
   g'(x) = 2x
   \]

5. **Combine Using the Chain Rule**:  
   Multiply the derivatives of the outer and inner functions:
   
   \[
   F'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x = -\frac{1}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \cdot 2x
   \]

6. **Simplify the Expression**:  
   Combine the terms to get the final derivative:
   
   \[
   F'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{2x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}
\]

   \]

The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using implicit differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. Let \( y = \tan^{-1} x \).
2. Then, \(\tan y = x\).
3. Differentiate both sides with respect to \(x\):
   - Left side: Using the chain rule, \(\frac{d}{dx} (\tan y) = \sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}\).
   - Right side: \(\frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1\).
4. This gives: 
   \[
   \sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
   \]
5. Solve for \(\frac{dy}{dx}\):
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2 y}
   \]
6. Recall the trigonometric identity: \(1 + \tan^2 y = \sec^2 y\).
7. Substitute \(\tan y = x\) into the identity:
   \[
   1 + x^2 = \sec^2 y
   \]
8. Substitute back into the derivative expression:
   \[

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u(x) = x \\
   v(x) = 1 + x^2
   \]

2. **Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u'(x) = 1 \\
   v'(x) = 2x
   \]

3. **Apply the Quotient Rule Formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the Numerator:**
   \[
   1(1 + x^2) - 2x^2 = 1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
   \]

5. **Write the Final Derivative:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{1 - x^{2}}{(1 + x^{2})^{2}}}

To find the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 2}} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Factor the Numerator:**
   
   Notice that \( x^2 - 4 \) is a difference of squares, which can be factored as:
   \[
   x^2 - 4 = (x + 2)(x - 2)
   \]

2. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression:
   \[
   \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2} = \frac{(x + 2)(x - 2)}{x - 2}
   \]
   
   Cancel out the common \( (x - 2) \) term in the numerator and denominator:
   \[
   \frac{(x + 2)(x - 2)}{x - 2} = x + 2
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   
   Now, compute the limit as \( x \) approaches 2 of the simplified expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 2}} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

\boxed{4}

To evaluate the limit:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Direct Substitution:**  
   If we directly substitute \( x = 0 \) into the expression, we get:
   \[
   \frac{\ln(1+0)}{0} = \frac{0}{0}
   \]
   This is an indeterminate form of type \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**  
   Since the limit results in an indeterminate form, we can apply L'Hospital's Rule. This rule states that for limits of the form \( \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), the limit can be evaluated by taking the derivative of the numerator and the derivative of the denominator separately.

   - **Derivative of the Numerator:**  
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} [\ln(1+x)] = \frac{1}{1+x}
     \]
   
   - **Derivative of the Denominator:**  
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[x] = 1
     \]

3. **Evaluate the New Limit:**
   After applying L'Hospital's Rule, the limit becomes:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{1+x}
   \]
   
4. **Substitute \( x = 0 \):**
   \[
   \frac{1}{1+0} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{1}

\lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x
\]

This expression is a well-known form that defines Euler's number \( e \). Specifically, it's recognized as one of the fundamental limits in calculus. 

As \( x \) approaches infinity:
- The term inside the parentheses, \( 1 + \frac{1}{x} \), approaches 1.
- The exponent \( x \) becomes very large.

This combination matches the definition of \( e \), which is:
\[
e = \lim_{n \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{n}\right)^n
\]

Here, as \( n \) (or in this case \( x \)) approaches infinity, the expression converges to \( e \). Thus, applying this understanding directly gives us:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{x}\right)^x = e
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The limit evaluates to the mathematical constant \( e \).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will use basic differentiation rules.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - For the term \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 3 \cdot 2x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
     
   - For the term \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - For the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives of all terms:**
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

\boxed{6x-5}

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \), we can use the **power rule** for differentiation, which states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Express the function in a different form:**
   
   \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} = x^{-1} \)

2. **Apply the power rule:**
   
   The derivative of \( x^n \) is \( n \cdot x^{n-1} \).
   
   Here, \( n = -1 \), so:
   
   \[
   f'(x) = (-1) \cdot x^{-1 - 1} = -x^{-2}
   \]

   Rewrite \( x^{-2} \) as \( \frac{1}{x^2} \).

   \[
   f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2}
   \]

The derivative of the function \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \) with respect to \( x \) is calculated by applying the power rule to each term. 

- The derivative of \( x^3 \) is \( 3x^2 \).

- The derivative of the constant term \( 1 \) is \( 0 \).

\[ f'(x) = 3x^2 + 4x + 1 \]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 3x^2 + 4x + 1 \).

To find the **second derivative** of the function \( F(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x \), we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative (\( F'(x) \))
We differentiate each term of \( F(x) \) with respect to \( x \):

\[
\begin{align*}
F(x) &= x^4 - 3x^3 + x \\
F'(x) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) - \frac{d}{dx}(3x^3) + \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
F'(x) &= 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
\end{align*}
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative (\( F''(x) \))
Now, we differentiate \( F'(x) \) with respect to \( x \):

\[
\begin{align*}
F'(x) &= 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1 \\
F''(x) &= \frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) - \frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) + \frac{d}{dx}(1) \\
F''(x) &= 12x^2 - 18x + 0 \\
F''(x) &= 12x^2 - 18x
\end{align*}
\]

The function f(x) = tan x can be differentiated using the quotient rule or by recalling standard derivatives.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express tan x as a fraction:**  
   tan x = sin x / cos x.

2. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**  
   If f(x) = u/v, then f'(x) = (v*u' - u*v') / v².

3. **Set u and v:**  
   Let u = sin x → u’ = cos x,  
   Let v = cos x → v’ = -sin x.

4. **Compute the derivative using the quotient rule:**  
   f'(x) = [cos x * cos x - sin x * (-sin x)] / (cos x)^2

5. **Simplify the numerator:**  
   = [cos²x + sin²x] / cos²x

6. **Use Pythagorean identity:**  
   cos²x + sin²x = 1.

7. **Final simplification:**  
   f'(x) = 1 / cos²x = sec²x.

**Answer:**  
The derivative of tan x is sec squared x.

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll use basic differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Rewrite the Function:**
   
   We can rewrite the function as:
   \[
   F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   
   The chain rule states that if \( F(x) = f(g(x)) \), then \( F'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \).

   - Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \).
   - Then, \( F(u) = u^{-1} \).

3. **Differentiate Each Part:**
   
   - Derivative of \( u = x^2 + 1 \):
     \[
     \frac{du}{dx} = 2x
     \]
     
   - Derivative of \( F(u) = u^{-1} \):
     \[
     \frac{dF}{du} = -u^{-2}
     \]

4. **Combine the Derivatives Using the Chain Rule:**
   
   Multiply the derivatives together:
   \[
   \frac{dF}{dx} = \frac{dF}{du} \times \frac{du}{dx} = (-u^{-2}) \times 2x
   \]

5. **Substitute Back \( u = x^2 + 1 \):**
   
   \[
   \frac{dF}{dx} = - (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \times 2x = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: }-\frac{2x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}

The derivative of F(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x) can be found using the quotient rule. 

Let u = e^x and v = 1 + e^x. Then:

- u’ = e^x
- v’ = e^x

Applying the quotient rule: F'(x) = (u’v - uv’) / v².

Substituting these values gives:
F'(x) = [e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)] / (1 + e^x)^2
= (e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x}) / (1 + e^x)^2
= e^x / (1 + e^x)^2.

Thus, the derivative simplifies to F'(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x)^2.

{Final Answer: "e^x/(1+e^x)^2"}

The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) is found using implicit differentiation and trigonometric identities.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. Let \( y = \tan^{-1} x \).
2. Then, \( \tan y = x \).
3. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side: Using the chain rule, \( \frac{d}{dx} [\tan y] = \sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \).
   - Right side: \( \frac{d}{dx} [x] = 1 \).
4. So, \( \sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \).
5. Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \): 
   - \( \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2 y} \).
6. Use the identity \( \sec^2 y = 1 + \tan^2 y \) and substitute \( \tan y = x \):
   - \( \sec^2 y = 1 + x^2 \).
7. Therefore, \( \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2} \).

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{1 + x^2}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the **product rule** for differentiation. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = f(x)g(x) \), then its derivative is:

\[
h'(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x)
\]

Applying this to \( F(x) = x \ln x \):

1. **Identify the functions:**
   - Let \( f(x) = x \)
   - Let \( g(x) = \ln x \)

2. **Find the derivatives of each function:**
   - \( f'(x) = 1 \) (since the derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1)
   - \( g'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) (since the derivative of \( \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \))

3. **Apply the product rule:**
\[
F'(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
\[
F'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{\ln x + 1}

The derivative of the function \(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\) with respect to \(x\) is found using the quotient rule:

1. Let \(u(x) = \sin x\), so \(u'(x) = \cos x\).
2. Let \(v(x) = x^2 + 1\), so \(v'(x) = 2x\).
3. Apply the quotient rule: \(\frac{d}{dx} \left( \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \right) = \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x (2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}\).

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative is \(\boxed{\dfrac{(x^2 + 1)\cos x - 2x\sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}\).

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), I start by recognizing that this is a composite function. The outer function is the exponential function, and the inner function is \( x^2 \).

I'll apply the chain rule, which states that the derivative of a composite function is the derivative of the outer function evaluated at the inner function multiplied by the derivative of the inner function.

First, I find the derivative of the outer function with respect to the inner function. The derivative of \( e^{u} \) with respect to \( u \) is simply \( e^{u} \).

Next, I find the derivative of the inner function \( x^2 \), which is \( 2x \).

Finally, I multiply these two results together to get the derivative of the original function.
</think>

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), we'll use the **Chain Rule**. The Chain Rule states that if you have a composite function \( f(g(x)) \), its derivative is:

\[
f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)
\]

In this case:
- The outer function \( f(u) = e^{u} \)
- The inner function \( u = x^2 \)

First, find the derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \):
\[
\frac{d}{du} e^{u} = e^{u}
\]

Next, find the derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \):
\[
\frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x
\]

Now, apply the Chain Rule by multiplying these two derivatives together:
\[
\frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \) is:

\[
F'(x) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{2 x e^{x^{2}}}
